For other uses, see <a href="/wiki/Albertus_(disambiguation)" class="mw-disambig" title="Albertus (disambiguation)">Albertus (disambiguation)</a>.</div>

href="https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Template:Catholic_philosophy&amp;action=edit"><abbr title="Edit this template">e</abbr></a></li></ul></div></td></tr></tbody></table>
<p><b>Albertus Magnus</b><sup id="cite_ref-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-6">&#91;a&#93;</a></sup> <span class="noexcerpt nowraplinks" style="font-size:85%; font-weight:normal;"><a href="/wiki/Dominican_Order" title="Dominican Order">OP</a></span> (<abbr title="circa">c.</abbr><span style="white-space:nowrap;">&#8201;1200</span> – 15 November 1280), also known as <b>Saint Albert the Great</b> or <b>Albert of Cologne</b>, was a German <a href="/wiki/Dominican_Order" title="Dominican Order">Dominican</a> <a href="/wiki/Friar" title="Friar">friar</a>, philosopher, scientist, and <a href="/wiki/Bishop_in_the_Catholic_Church" class="mw-redirect" title="Bishop in the Catholic Church">bishop</a>. Later <a href="/wiki/Canonization" title="Canonization">canonized</a> as a <a href="/wiki/Catholic_saint" class="mw-redirect" title="Catholic saint">Catholic saint</a>, he was known during his lifetime as <i>Doctor universalis</i> and <i>Doctor expertus</i> and, late in his life, the <a href="/wiki/Sobriquet" title="Sobriquet">sobriquet</a> <i><a href="/wiki/List_of_people_known_as_%22the_Great%22" class="mw-redirect" title="List of people known as &quot;the Great&quot;">Magnus</a></i> was appended to his name.<sup id="cite_ref-7" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-7">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> Scholars such as James A. Weisheipl and Joachim R. Söder have referred to him as the greatest German philosopher and theologian of the <a href="/wiki/Middle_Ages" title="Middle Ages">Middle Ages</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-8" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-8">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup> The <a href="/wiki/Catholic_Church" title="Catholic Church">Catholic Church</a> distinguishes him as one of the 37 <a href="/wiki/Doctor_of_the_Church" title="Doctor of the Church">Doctors of the Church</a>.
</p>
<meta property="mw:PageProp/toc" />
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Biography">Biography</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Albertus_Magnus&amp;action=edit&amp;section=1" title="Edit section: Biography">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>It seems likely that Albert was born sometime before 1200, given well-attested evidence that he was aged over 80 on his death in 1280.<sup id="cite_ref-:0_9-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:0-9">&#91;8&#93;</a></sup> Two later sources say that Albert was about 87 on his death, which has led 1193 to be commonly given as the date of Albert's birth, but this information does not have enough evidence to be confirmed.<sup id="cite_ref-:0_9-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:0-9">&#91;8&#93;</a></sup> Albert was probably born in Lauingen (now in <a href="/wiki/Duchy_of_Bavaria" title="Duchy of Bavaria">Bavaria</a>), since he called himself 'Albert of Lauingen', but this might simply be a family name. Most probably his family was of <i>ministerial</i> class; his familiar connection with (being son of the count) the Bollstädt noble family is almost certainly mere conjecture by 15th century <a href="/wiki/Hagiography" title="Hagiography">hagiographers</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-:0_9-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:0-9">&#91;8&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Albert was probably educated principally at the <a href="/wiki/University_of_Padua" title="University of Padua">University of Padua</a>, where he received instruction in <a href="/wiki/Aristotle" title="Aristotle">Aristotle</a>'s writings. A late account by Rudolph de Novamagia refers to Albertus' encounter with the <a href="/wiki/Blessed_Virgin_Mary" class="mw-redirect" title="Blessed Virgin Mary">Blessed Virgin Mary</a>, who convinced him to enter <a href="/wiki/Holy_Orders" class="mw-redirect" title="Holy Orders">Holy Orders</a>. In 1223 (or 1229)<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETugwell19884–5_10-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETugwell19884–5-10">&#91;9&#93;</a></sup> he became a member of the <a href="/wiki/Dominican_Order" title="Dominican Order">Dominican Order</a>, and studied <a href="/wiki/Theology" title="Theology">theology</a> at <a href="/wiki/Bologna" title="Bologna">Bologna</a> and elsewhere. Selected to fill the position of lecturer at Cologne, Germany, where the Dominicans had a house, he taught for several years there, as well as in <a href="/wiki/Regensburg" title="Regensburg">Regensburg</a>, <a href="/wiki/Freiburg" class="mw-redirect" title="Freiburg">Freiburg</a>, <a href="/wiki/Strasbourg" title="Strasbourg">Strasbourg</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Hildesheim" title="Hildesheim">Hildesheim</a>. During his first tenure as lecturer at Cologne, Albert wrote his <i>Summa de bono</i> after discussion with <a href="/wiki/Philip_the_Chancellor" title="Philip the Chancellor">Philip the Chancellor</a> concerning the transcendental properties of being.<sup id="cite_ref-Kovach,_Francs_1980,_p.X_11-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Kovach,_Francs_1980,_p.X-11">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup> In 1245, Albert became <a href="/wiki/Master_of_theology" class="mw-redirect" title="Master of theology">master of theology</a> under Gueric of Saint-Quentin, the first German Dominican to achieve this distinction. Following this turn of events, Albert was able to teach theology at the <a href="/wiki/University_of_Paris" title="University of Paris">University of Paris</a> as a full-time professor, holding the seat of the Chair of Theology at the College of St. James.<sup id="cite_ref-Kovach,_Francs_1980,_p.X_11-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Kovach,_Francs_1980,_p.X-11">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup> During this time <a href="/wiki/Thomas_Aquinas" title="Thomas Aquinas">Thomas Aquinas</a> began to study under Albertus.<sup id="cite_ref-Kennedy_12-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Kennedy-12">&#91;11&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Vincenzo_onofri,_sant%27alberto_magno,_1493.JPG" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5c/Vincenzo_onofri%2C_sant%27alberto_magno%2C_1493.JPG/220px-Vincenzo_onofri%2C_sant%27alberto_magno%2C_1493.JPG" decoding="async" width="220" height="184" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5c/Vincenzo_onofri%2C_sant%27alberto_magno%2C_1493.JPG/330px-Vincenzo_onofri%2C_sant%27alberto_magno%2C_1493.JPG 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5c/Vincenzo_onofri%2C_sant%27alberto_magno%2C_1493.JPG/440px-Vincenzo_onofri%2C_sant%27alberto_magno%2C_1493.JPG 2x" data-file-width="1791" data-file-height="1500" /></a><figcaption>Bust of Albertus Magnus by <a href="/wiki/Vincenzo_Onofri" title="Vincenzo Onofri">Vincenzo Onofri</a>, <abbr title="circa">c.</abbr><span style="white-space:nowrap;">&#8201;1493</span></figcaption></figure>
<p>Albert was the first to comment on virtually all of the writings of <a href="/wiki/Aristotle" title="Aristotle">Aristotle</a>, thus making them accessible to wider academic debate. The study of Aristotle brought him to study and comment on the teachings of Muslim academics, notably <a href="/wiki/Avicenna" title="Avicenna">Avicenna</a> and <a href="/wiki/Averroes" title="Averroes">Averroes</a>, and this would bring him into the heart of academic debate.
</p><p>In 1254 Albert was made <a href="/wiki/Provincial_superior" title="Provincial superior">provincial</a> of the Dominican Order,<sup id="cite_ref-Kennedy_12-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Kennedy-12">&#91;11&#93;</a></sup> and fulfilled the duties of the office with great care and efficiency. During his tenure he publicly defended the Dominicans against attacks by the secular and regular faculty of the University of Paris, commented on <a href="/wiki/John_the_Evangelist" title="John the Evangelist">John the Evangelist</a>, and answered what he perceived as errors of the <a href="/wiki/Early_Islamic_philosophy" title="Early Islamic philosophy">Islamic philosopher</a> Averroes.
</p><p>In 1259 Albert took part in the General Chapter of the Dominicans at <a href="/wiki/Valenciennes" title="Valenciennes">Valenciennes</a> together with Thomas Aquinas, masters Bonushomo Britto,<sup id="cite_ref-13" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-13">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup> Florentius,<sup id="cite_ref-14" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-14">&#91;13&#93;</a></sup> and Peter (later <a href="/wiki/Pope_Innocent_V" title="Pope Innocent V">Pope Innocent V</a>) establishing a <i>ratio studiorum</i> or program of studies for the Dominicans<sup id="cite_ref-15" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-15">&#91;14&#93;</a></sup> that featured the study of philosophy as an innovation for those not sufficiently trained to study theology. This innovation initiated the tradition of Dominican scholastic philosophy put into practice, for example, in 1265 at the Order's <i><a href="/wiki/Studium_provinciale" class="mw-redirect" title="Studium provinciale">studium provinciale</a></i> at the convent of <a href="/wiki/Santa_Sabina" title="Santa Sabina">Santa Sabina</a> in Rome, out of which would develop the <a href="/wiki/Pontifical_University_of_Saint_Thomas_Aquinas" title="Pontifical University of Saint Thomas Aquinas">Pontifical University of Saint Thomas Aquinas</a>, the "Angelicum".<sup id="cite_ref-16" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-16">&#91;15&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:2004_K%C3%B6ln_Sarkophag_Albertus_Magnus.JPG" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b4/2004_K%C3%B6ln_Sarkophag_Albertus_Magnus.JPG/220px-2004_K%C3%B6ln_Sarkophag_Albertus_Magnus.JPG" decoding="async" width="220" height="147" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b4/2004_K%C3%B6ln_Sarkophag_Albertus_Magnus.JPG/330px-2004_K%C3%B6ln_Sarkophag_Albertus_Magnus.JPG 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b4/2004_K%C3%B6ln_Sarkophag_Albertus_Magnus.JPG/440px-2004_K%C3%B6ln_Sarkophag_Albertus_Magnus.JPG 2x" data-file-width="1536" data-file-height="1024" /></a><figcaption>Roman <a href="/wiki/Sarcophagus" title="Sarcophagus">sarcophagus</a> containing the relics of Albertus Magnus in the crypt of <a href="/wiki/St._Andrew%27s_Church,_Cologne" title="St. Andrew&#39;s Church, Cologne">St. Andrew's Church, Cologne</a>, Germany</figcaption></figure>
<p>In 1260 <a href="/wiki/Pope_Alexander_IV" title="Pope Alexander IV">Pope Alexander IV</a> made him <a href="/wiki/Bishop_of_Regensburg" class="mw-redirect" title="Bishop of Regensburg">bishop of Regensburg</a>, an office from which he resigned after three years. During the exercise of his duties he enhanced his reputation for humility by refusing to ride a horse, in accord with the dictates of the Order, instead traversing his huge diocese on foot. In 1263 <a href="/wiki/Pope_Urban_IV" title="Pope Urban IV">Pope Urban IV</a> relieved him of the duties of bishop and asked him to preach the <a href="/wiki/Eighth_Crusade" title="Eighth Crusade">eighth Crusade</a> in German-speaking countries.<sup id="cite_ref-Markus_17-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Markus-17">&#91;16&#93;</a></sup> After this, he was especially known for acting as a mediator between conflicting parties. In Cologne he is known not only for being the founder of Germany's oldest university there, but also for "the big verdict" (der Große Schied) of 1258, which brought an end to the conflict between the citizens of Cologne and the archbishop. Among the last of his labors was the defense of the orthodoxy of his former pupil, Thomas Aquinas, whose death in 1274 grieved Albert (the story that he travelled to Paris in person to defend the teachings of Aquinas can not be confirmed).
</p><p>Albert was a scientist, philosopher, astrologer, theologian, spiritual writer, ecumenist, and diplomat. Under the auspices of Humbert of Romans, Albert molded the curriculum of studies for all Dominican students, introduced Aristotle to the classroom and probed the work of <a href="/wiki/Neoplatonists" class="mw-redirect" title="Neoplatonists">Neoplatonists</a>, such as <a href="/wiki/Plotinus" title="Plotinus">Plotinus</a>. Indeed, it was the thirty years of work done by Aquinas and himself that allowed for the inclusion of Aristotelian study in the curriculum of Dominican schools.
</p><p>After suffering a collapse of health in 1278, he died on 15 November 1280 in the Dominican convent in Cologne, Germany. Since 15 November 1954 his relics are in a Roman sarcophagus in the <a href="/wiki/Crypt" title="Crypt">crypt</a> of the Dominican <a href="/wiki/St._Andrew%27s_Church,_Cologne" title="St. Andrew&#39;s Church, Cologne">St. Andrew's Church</a> in Cologne.<sup id="cite_ref-18" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-18">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup> Although his body was claimed to be <a href="/wiki/Incorruptibility" title="Incorruptibility">incorrupt</a> at the first exhumation three years after his death, at the exhumation in 1483 only a <a href="/wiki/Skeleton" title="Skeleton">skeleton</a> remained.<sup id="cite_ref-Cruz_1977_19-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Cruz_1977-19">&#91;18&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Albert was <a href="/wiki/Beatified" class="mw-redirect" title="Beatified">beatified</a> in 1622. He was <a href="/wiki/Canonized" class="mw-redirect" title="Canonized">canonized</a> and proclaimed a Doctor of the Church on 16 December 1931 by <a href="/wiki/Pope_Pius_XI" title="Pope Pius XI">Pope Pius XI</a><sup id="cite_ref-Markus_17-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Markus-17">&#91;16&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-20" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-20">&#91;19&#93;</a></sup> and the patron saint of natural scientists in 1941. St. Albert's <a href="/wiki/Feast_day" class="mw-redirect" title="Feast day">feast day</a> is November 15.
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Writings">Writings</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Albertus_Magnus&amp;action=edit&amp;section=2" title="Edit section: Writings">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Albertus_Magnus-Denkmal.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/04/Albertus_Magnus-Denkmal.jpg/170px-Albertus_Magnus-Denkmal.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="227" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/04/Albertus_Magnus-Denkmal.jpg/255px-Albertus_Magnus-Denkmal.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/04/Albertus_Magnus-Denkmal.jpg/340px-Albertus_Magnus-Denkmal.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1224" data-file-height="1632" /></a><figcaption>Albertus Magnus monument at the <a href="/wiki/University_of_Cologne" title="University of Cologne">University of Cologne</a></figcaption></figure>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:AlbertusMagnus.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d0/AlbertusMagnus.jpg/220px-AlbertusMagnus.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="232" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/d/d0/AlbertusMagnus.jpg 1.5x" data-file-width="285" data-file-height="300" /></a><figcaption><i>Saint Albertus Magnus</i>, a fresco by <a href="/wiki/Tommaso_da_Modena" title="Tommaso da Modena">Tommaso da Modena</a> (1352), Church of San Nicolò, <a href="/wiki/Treviso" title="Treviso">Treviso</a>, Italy</figcaption></figure>
<p>Albert's writings collected in 1899 went to thirty-eight volumes. These displayed his prolific habits and encyclopedic knowledge of topics such as <a href="/wiki/Logic" title="Logic">logic</a>, <a href="/wiki/Theology" title="Theology">theology</a>, <a href="/wiki/Botany" title="Botany">botany</a>, <a href="/wiki/Geography" title="Geography">geography</a>, <a href="/wiki/Astronomy" title="Astronomy">astronomy</a>, <a href="/wiki/Astrology" title="Astrology">astrology</a>, <a href="/wiki/Mineralogy" title="Mineralogy">mineralogy</a>, <a href="/wiki/Alchemy" title="Alchemy">alchemy</a>, <a href="/wiki/Zoology" title="Zoology">zoology</a>, <a href="/wiki/Physiology" title="Physiology">physiology</a>, <a href="/wiki/Phrenology" title="Phrenology">phrenology</a>, <a href="/wiki/Justice" title="Justice">justice</a>, <a href="/wiki/Law" title="Law">law</a>, <a href="/wiki/Friendship" title="Friendship">friendship</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Love" title="Love">love</a>. He digested, interpreted, and systematized the whole of Aristotle's works, gleaned from the Latin translations and notes of the Arabian commentators, in accordance with Church doctrine. Most modern knowledge of Aristotle was preserved and presented by Albert.<sup id="cite_ref-Kennedy_12-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Kennedy-12">&#91;11&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>His principal theological works are a commentary in three volumes on the Books of the Sentences of <a href="/wiki/Peter_Lombard" title="Peter Lombard">Peter Lombard</a> (<i>Magister Sententiarum</i>), and the <i><a href="/wiki/Summa_Theologiae" class="mw-redirect" title="Summa Theologiae">Summa Theologiae</a></i> in two volumes. The latter is in substance a more didactic repetition of the former.
</p><p>Albert's activity, however, was more philosophical than theological (see <a href="/wiki/Scholasticism" title="Scholasticism">Scholasticism</a>). The philosophical works, occupying the first six and the last of the 21 volumes, are generally divided according to the <a href="/wiki/Aristotelianism" title="Aristotelianism">Aristotelian</a> scheme of the sciences, and consist of interpretations and condensations of Aristotle's relative works, with supplementary discussions upon contemporary topics, and occasional divergences from the opinions of the master. Albert believed that Aristotle's approach to natural philosophy did not pose any obstacle to the development of a Christian philosophical view of the natural order.<sup id="cite_ref-Markus_17-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Markus-17">&#91;16&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Firenze,_alberto_magno,_de_animalibus,_1450-1500_ca._cod_fiesolano_67,_01.JPG" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/1a/Firenze%2C_alberto_magno%2C_de_animalibus%2C_1450-1500_ca._cod_fiesolano_67%2C_01.JPG/170px-Firenze%2C_alberto_magno%2C_de_animalibus%2C_1450-1500_ca._cod_fiesolano_67%2C_01.JPG" decoding="async" width="170" height="121" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/1a/Firenze%2C_alberto_magno%2C_de_animalibus%2C_1450-1500_ca._cod_fiesolano_67%2C_01.JPG/255px-Firenze%2C_alberto_magno%2C_de_animalibus%2C_1450-1500_ca._cod_fiesolano_67%2C_01.JPG 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/1a/Firenze%2C_alberto_magno%2C_de_animalibus%2C_1450-1500_ca._cod_fiesolano_67%2C_01.JPG/340px-Firenze%2C_alberto_magno%2C_de_animalibus%2C_1450-1500_ca._cod_fiesolano_67%2C_01.JPG 2x" data-file-width="2676" data-file-height="1908" /></a><figcaption><i>De animalibus</i> (c. 1450–1500, cod. fiesolano 67, <a href="/wiki/Biblioteca_Medicea_Laurenziana" class="mw-redirect" title="Biblioteca Medicea Laurenziana">Biblioteca Medicea Laurenziana</a>)</figcaption></figure>
<p>Albert's knowledge of natural science was considerable and for the age remarkably accurate. His industry in every department was great: not only did he produce commentaries and paraphrases of the entire Aristotelian corpus, including his scientific works, but Albert also added to and improved upon them. His books on topics like botany, zoology, and minerals included information from ancient sources, but also results of his own empirical investigations. These investigations pushed several of the special sciences forward, beyond the reliance on classical texts. In the case of embryology, for example, it has been claimed that little of value was written between Aristotle and Albert, who managed to identify organs within eggs.<sup id="cite_ref-21" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-21">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup> Furthermore, Albert also effectively invented entire special sciences, where Aristotle has not covered a topic. For example, prior to Albert, there was no systematic study of minerals.<sup id="cite_ref-:2_22-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:2-22">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup> For the breadth of these achievements, he was bestowed the name <i>Doctor Universalis.</i>
</p><p>Much of Albert's empirical contributions to the natural sciences have been superseded, but his general approach to science may be surprisingly modern. For example, in <i>De Mineralibus</i> (Book II, Tractate ii, Ch. 1) Albert claims, "For it is [the task] of natural science not simply to accept what we are told but to inquire into the causes of natural things."<sup id="cite_ref-:2_22-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:2-22">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Alchemy">Alchemy</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Albertus_Magnus&amp;action=edit&amp;section=3" title="Edit section: Alchemy">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Liebig_Company_Trading_Card_Ad_01.12.003_front.tif" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/81/Liebig_Company_Trading_Card_Ad_01.12.003_front.tif/lossy-page1-170px-Liebig_Company_Trading_Card_Ad_01.12.003_front.tif.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="107" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/81/Liebig_Company_Trading_Card_Ad_01.12.003_front.tif/lossy-page1-255px-Liebig_Company_Trading_Card_Ad_01.12.003_front.tif.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/81/Liebig_Company_Trading_Card_Ad_01.12.003_front.tif/lossy-page1-340px-Liebig_Company_Trading_Card_Ad_01.12.003_front.tif.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1305" data-file-height="824" /></a><figcaption>Albertus Magnus, Chimistes Celebres, <a href="/wiki/Liebig%27s_Extract_of_Meat_Company" title="Liebig&#39;s Extract of Meat Company">Liebig's Extract of Meat Company</a> Trading Card, 1929</figcaption></figure>
<p>In the centuries since his death, many stories arose about Albert as an <a href="/wiki/Alchemy" title="Alchemy">alchemist</a> and magician. "Much of the modern confusion results from the fact that later works, particularly the alchemical work known as the <i>Secreta Alberti</i> or the <i>Experimenta Alberti</i>, were falsely attributed to Albertus by their authors to increase the prestige of the text through association."<sup id="cite_ref-23" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-23">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup> On the subject of alchemy and chemistry, many treatises relating to alchemy have been attributed to him, though in his authentic writings he had little to say on the subject, and then mostly through commentary on Aristotle. For example, in his commentary, <i>De mineralibus</i>, he refers to the power of stones, but does not elaborate on what these powers might be.<sup id="cite_ref-24" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-24">&#91;23&#93;</a></sup> A wide range of Pseudo-Albertine works dealing with alchemy exist, though, showing the belief developed in the generations following Albert's death that he had mastered alchemy, one of the fundamental sciences of the Middle Ages. These include <i>Metals and Materials</i>; the <i>Secrets of Chemistry</i>; the <i>Origin of Metals</i>; the <i>Origins of Compounds</i>, and a <i>Concordance </i>which is a collection of <i>Observations on the <a href="/wiki/Philosopher%27s_stone" title="Philosopher&#39;s stone">philosopher's stone</a></i>; and other alchemy-chemistry topics, collected under the name of <i>Theatrum Chemicum</i>.<sup id="cite_ref-25" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-25">&#91;24&#93;</a></sup> He is credited with the discovery of the element <a href="/wiki/Arsenic" title="Arsenic">arsenic</a><sup id="cite_ref-BuildingBlocks451-3_26-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-BuildingBlocks451-3-26">&#91;25&#93;</a></sup> and experimented with photosensitive chemicals, including <a href="/wiki/Silver_nitrate" title="Silver nitrate">silver nitrate</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-27" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-27">&#91;26&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-28" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-28">&#91;27&#93;</a></sup> He did believe that stones had occult properties, as he related in his work <i>De mineralibus</i>. However, there is scant evidence that he personally performed alchemical experiments.
</p><p>According to legend, Albert is said to have discovered the philosopher's stone and passed it on to his pupil Thomas Aquinas, shortly before his death. Albert does not confirm he discovered the stone in his writings, but he did record that he witnessed the creation of gold by "transmutation."<sup id="cite_ref-29" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-29">&#91;28&#93;</a></sup> Given that Thomas Aquinas died six years before Albert's death, this legend as stated is unlikely.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Astronomy">Astronomy</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Albertus_Magnus&amp;action=edit&amp;section=4" title="Edit section: Astronomy">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Albert was deeply interested in <a href="/wiki/Astronomy" title="Astronomy">astronomy</a>, as has been articulated by scholars such as Paola Zambelli<sup id="cite_ref-30" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-30">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup> and Scott Hendrix.<sup id="cite_ref-:1_31-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:1-31">&#91;30&#93;</a></sup> Throughout the Middle Ages&#160;–and well into the early modern period– astrology was widely accepted by scientists and intellectuals who held the view that life on earth is effectively a microcosm within the macrocosm (the latter being the cosmos itself). It was believed that correspondence therefore exists between the two and thus the celestial bodies follow patterns and cycles analogous to those on earth. With this worldview, it seemed reasonable to assert that astrology could be used to predict the probable future of a human being. Albert argued that an understanding of the celestial influences affecting us could help us to live our lives more in accord with Christian precepts.<sup id="cite_ref-:1_31-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:1-31">&#91;30&#93;</a></sup> The most comprehensive statement of his astrological beliefs is to be found in two separates works that he authored around 1260, known as the <i><a href="/wiki/Speculum_astronomiae" class="mw-redirect" title="Speculum astronomiae">Speculum astronomiae</a></i> and the <a href="/wiki/De_Fato" class="mw-redirect" title="De Fato">De Fato</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-32" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-32">&#91;31&#93;</a></sup> However, details of these beliefs can be found in almost everything he wrote, from his early <i>De natura boni</i> to his last work, the <i>Summa theologiae</i>.<sup id="cite_ref-33" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-33">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup> His <i>speculum</i> was critiqued by <a href="/wiki/Gerard_of_Silteo" title="Gerard of Silteo">Gerard of Silteo</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-34" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-34">&#91;33&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<ul class="gallery mw-gallery-traditional">
		<li class="gallerybox" style="width: 155px">
			<div class="thumb" style="width: 150px; height: 150px;"><span typeof="mw:File"><a href="/wiki/File:Albertus_Magnus_%E2%80%93_De_meteoris,_1488_%E2%80%93_BEIC_13302626.jpg" class="mw-file-description" title="De meteoris, 1488"><img alt="De meteoris, 1488" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/ba/Albertus_Magnus_%E2%80%93_De_meteoris%2C_1488_%E2%80%93_BEIC_13302626.jpg/86px-Albertus_Magnus_%E2%80%93_De_meteoris%2C_1488_%E2%80%93_BEIC_13302626.jpg" decoding="async" width="86" height="120" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/ba/Albertus_Magnus_%E2%80%93_De_meteoris%2C_1488_%E2%80%93_BEIC_13302626.jpg/128px-Albertus_Magnus_%E2%80%93_De_meteoris%2C_1488_%E2%80%93_BEIC_13302626.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/ba/Albertus_Magnus_%E2%80%93_De_meteoris%2C_1488_%E2%80%93_BEIC_13302626.jpg/171px-Albertus_Magnus_%E2%80%93_De_meteoris%2C_1488_%E2%80%93_BEIC_13302626.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1329" data-file-height="1861" /></a></span></div>
			<div class="gallerytext">
<p><i>De meteoris</i>, 1488
</p>
			</div>
		</li>
</ul>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Matter_and_form">Matter and form</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Albertus_Magnus&amp;action=edit&amp;section=5" title="Edit section: Matter and form">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Albert believed that all natural things were compositions of matter and form, he referred to it as <i>quod est</i> and <i>quo est</i>. Albert also believed that God alone is the absolute ruling entity. Albert's version of <a href="/wiki/Hylomorphism" title="Hylomorphism">hylomorphism</a> is very similar to the <a href="/wiki/Aristotelianism" title="Aristotelianism">Aristotelian</a> doctrine.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Music">Music</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Albertus_Magnus&amp;action=edit&amp;section=6" title="Edit section: Music">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Albert is known for his commentary on the musical practice of his times. Most of his written musical observations are found in his commentary on Aristotle's <i>Poetics</i>. He rejected the idea of "<a href="/wiki/Musica_universalis" title="Musica universalis">music of the spheres</a>" as ridiculous: movement of astronomical bodies, he supposed, is incapable of generating sound. He wrote extensively on proportions in music, and on the three different subjective levels on which <a href="/wiki/Plainchant" class="mw-redirect" title="Plainchant">plainchant</a> could work on the human soul: purging of the impure; illumination leading to contemplation; and nourishing perfection through contemplation. Of particular interest to 20th-century music theorists is the attention he paid to silence as an integral part of music.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Metaphysics_of_morals">Metaphysics of morals</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Albertus_Magnus&amp;action=edit&amp;section=7" title="Edit section: Metaphysics of morals">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Both of his early treatises, <i>De natura boni</i> and <i>De bono</i>, start with a metaphysical investigation into the concepts of the good in general and the physical good. Albert refers to the physical good as <i>bonum naturae</i>. Albert does this before directly dealing with the moral concepts of metaphysics. In Albert's later works, he says in order to understand human or moral goodness, the individual must first recognize what it means to be good and do good deeds. This procedure reflects Albert's preoccupations with neo-Platonic theories of good as well as the doctrines of <a href="/wiki/Pseudo-Dionysius" class="mw-redirect" title="Pseudo-Dionysius">Pseudo-Dionysius</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-35" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-35">&#91;34&#93;</a></sup> Albert's view was highly valued by the Catholic Church and his peers.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Natural_law">Natural law</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Albertus_Magnus&amp;action=edit&amp;section=8" title="Edit section: Natural law">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Albert devoted the last tractatus of <i>De Bono</i> to a theory of justice and <a href="/wiki/Natural_law" title="Natural law">natural law</a>. Albert places God as the pinnacle of justice and natural law. God legislates and divine authority is supreme. Up until his time, it was the only work specifically devoted to natural law written by a theologian or philosopher.<sup id="cite_ref-36" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-36">&#91;35&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Friendship">Friendship</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Albertus_Magnus&amp;action=edit&amp;section=9" title="Edit section: Friendship">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Albert mentions friendship in his work, <i>De bono</i>, as well as presenting his ideals and morals of friendship in the very beginning of <i>Tractatus II</i>. Later in his life he published <i>Super Ethica</i>.<sup id="cite_ref-37" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-37">&#91;36&#93;</a></sup> With his development of friendship throughout his work it is evident that friendship ideals and morals took relevance as his life went on. Albert comments on Aristotle's view of friendship with a quote from <a href="/wiki/Cicero" title="Cicero">Cicero</a>, who writes, "friendship is nothing other than the harmony between things divine and human, with goodwill and love". Albert agrees with this commentary but he also adds in harmony or agreement.<sup id="cite_ref-38" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-38">&#91;37&#93;</a></sup> Albert calls this harmony, <i>consensio</i>, itself a certain kind of movement within the human spirit. Albert fully agrees with Aristotle in the sense that friendship is a virtue. Albert relates the inherent metaphysical contentedness between friendship and moral goodness. Albert describes several levels of goodness; the useful (<i>utile</i>), the pleasurable (<i>delectabile</i>) and the authentic or unqualified good (<i>honestum</i>). Then in turn there are three levels of friendship based on each of those levels, namely friendship based on usefulness (<i>amicitia utilis</i>), friendship based on pleasure (<i>amicitia delectabilis</i>), and friendship rooted in unqualified goodness (<i>amicitia honesti</i>; <i>amicitia quae fundatur super honestum</i>).<sup id="cite_ref-39" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-39">&#91;38&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Cultural_references">Cultural references</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Albertus_Magnus&amp;action=edit&amp;section=10" title="Edit section: Cultural references">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:France_Strasbourg_Cathedral_Tympanum.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/bf/France_Strasbourg_Cathedral_Tympanum.jpg/220px-France_Strasbourg_Cathedral_Tympanum.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="150" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/bf/France_Strasbourg_Cathedral_Tympanum.jpg/330px-France_Strasbourg_Cathedral_Tympanum.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/bf/France_Strasbourg_Cathedral_Tympanum.jpg/440px-France_Strasbourg_Cathedral_Tympanum.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1524" data-file-height="1040" /></a><figcaption>The <a href="/wiki/Tympanum_(architecture)" title="Tympanum (architecture)">tympanum</a> and <a href="/wiki/Archivolt" title="Archivolt">archivolts</a> of <a href="/wiki/Strasbourg_Cathedral" title="Strasbourg Cathedral">Strasbourg Cathedral</a>, with iconography inspired by Albertus Magnus</figcaption></figure>
<p>The <a href="/wiki/Iconography" title="Iconography">iconography</a> of the <a href="/wiki/Pediment" title="Pediment">tympanum</a> and <a href="/wiki/Archivolt" title="Archivolt">archivolts</a> of the late 13th-century <a href="/wiki/Gate" title="Gate">portal</a> of <a href="/wiki/Notre_Dame_de_Strasbourg" class="mw-redirect" title="Notre Dame de Strasbourg">Strasbourg Cathedral</a> was inspired by Albert's writings.<sup id="cite_ref-40" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-40">&#91;39&#93;</a></sup> Albert is frequently mentioned by <a href="/wiki/Dante_Alighieri" title="Dante Alighieri">Dante</a>, who made his doctrine of <a href="/wiki/Free_will" title="Free will">free will</a> the basis of his ethical system. In his <i><a href="/wiki/Divine_Comedy" title="Divine Comedy">Divine Comedy</a></i>, Dante places Albertus with his pupil Thomas Aquinas among the great lovers of wisdom (<i>Spiriti Sapienti</i>) in the Heaven of the Sun.
</p><p>In <i><a href="/wiki/The_Concept_of_Anxiety" title="The Concept of Anxiety">The Concept of Anxiety</a></i>, <a href="/wiki/S%C3%B8ren_Kierkegaard" title="Søren Kierkegaard">Søren Kierkegaard</a> wrote that Albert, "arrogantly boasted of his speculation before the deity and suddenly became stupid." Kierkegaard cites Gotthard Oswald Marbach whom he quotes as saying "<i>Albertus repente ex asino factus philosophus et ex philosopho asinus</i>" [Albert was suddenly transformed from an ass into a philosopher and from a philosopher into an ass].<sup id="cite_ref-41" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-41">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In <a href="/wiki/Mary_Shelley" title="Mary Shelley">Mary Shelley's</a> <i><a href="/wiki/Frankenstein" title="Frankenstein">Frankenstein</a></i>, the titular Frankenstein studies the works of Albertus Magnus.<sup id="cite_ref-42" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-42">&#91;41&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p><a href="/wiki/Johann_Eduard_Erdmann" title="Johann Eduard Erdmann">Johann Eduard Erdmann</a> considers Albert greater and more original than his pupil <a href="/wiki/Aquinas" class="mw-redirect" title="Aquinas">Aquinas</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-43" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-43">&#91;42&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In <a href="/wiki/Open_All_Hours" title="Open All Hours">Open All Hours</a>, <a href="/wiki/Arkwright_(Open_All_Hours)" class="mw-redirect" title="Arkwright (Open All Hours)">Arkwright</a> invents St Albert's day so <a href="/wiki/Granville_(Open_All_Hours)" class="mw-redirect" title="Granville (Open All Hours)">Grandville</a> can check customers pockets.<sup id="cite_ref-44" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-44">&#91;43&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Influence_and_tribute">Influence and tribute</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Albertus_Magnus&amp;action=edit&amp;section=11" title="Edit section: Influence and tribute">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Albertus_Magnus_Painting_by_Joos_van_Gent.jpeg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/83/Albertus_Magnus_Painting_by_Joos_van_Gent.jpeg/170px-Albertus_Magnus_Painting_by_Joos_van_Gent.jpeg" decoding="async" width="170" height="308" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/8/83/Albertus_Magnus_Painting_by_Joos_van_Gent.jpeg 1.5x" data-file-width="227" data-file-height="411" /></a><figcaption>Painting by <a href="/wiki/Joos_van_Wassenhove" class="mw-redirect" title="Joos van Wassenhove">Joos (Justus) van Gent</a>, <a href="/wiki/Urbino" title="Urbino">Urbino</a>, c. 1475</figcaption></figure>
<p>A number of schools have been named after Albert, including <a href="/wiki/Albertus_Magnus_High_School" title="Albertus Magnus High School">Albertus Magnus High School</a> in <a href="/wiki/Bardonia,_New_York" title="Bardonia, New York">Bardonia, New York</a>;<sup id="cite_ref-45" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-45">&#91;44&#93;</a></sup> Albertus Magnus Lyceum in <a href="/wiki/River_Forest,_Illinois" title="River Forest, Illinois">River Forest, Illinois</a>; and <a href="/wiki/Albertus_Magnus_College" title="Albertus Magnus College">Albertus Magnus College</a> in <a href="/wiki/New_Haven,_Connecticut" title="New Haven, Connecticut">New Haven, Connecticut</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-46" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-46">&#91;45&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Albertus Magnus Science Hall at <a href="/wiki/Thomas_Aquinas_College" title="Thomas Aquinas College">Thomas Aquinas College</a>, in Santa Paula, California, is named in honor of Albert. The main science buildings at <a href="/wiki/Providence_College" title="Providence College">Providence College</a> and <a href="/wiki/Aquinas_College_(Michigan)" title="Aquinas College (Michigan)">Aquinas College</a> in Grand Rapids, Michigan, are also named after him.
</p><p>The central square at the campus of the <a href="/wiki/University_of_Cologne" title="University of Cologne">University of Cologne</a> features a statue of Albert and is named after him.
</p><p>The <a href="/wiki/Academy_for_Science_and_Design" title="Academy for Science and Design">Academy for Science and Design</a> in <a href="/wiki/New_Hampshire" title="New Hampshire">New Hampshire</a> honored Albert by naming one of its four houses Magnus House.
</p><p>As a tribute to the scholar's contributions to the law, the <a href="/wiki/University_of_Houston_Law_Center" title="University of Houston Law Center">University of Houston Law Center</a> displays a statue of Albert. It is located on the campus of the <a href="/wiki/University_of_Houston" title="University of Houston">University of Houston</a>.
</p><p>The Albertus-Magnus-Gymnasium is found in Rottweil, Germany.
</p><p>In <a href="/wiki/Managua" title="Managua">Managua</a>, Nicaragua, the Albertus Magnus International Institute, a business and economic development research center, was founded in 2004.
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:University_of_Santo_Tomas.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f5/University_of_Santo_Tomas.jpg/220px-University_of_Santo_Tomas.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="150" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f5/University_of_Santo_Tomas.jpg/330px-University_of_Santo_Tomas.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f5/University_of_Santo_Tomas.jpg/440px-University_of_Santo_Tomas.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1000" data-file-height="682" /></a><figcaption>University of Santo Tomas in the Philippines</figcaption></figure>
<p>In the Philippines, the Albertus Magnus Building at the <a href="/wiki/University_of_Santo_Tomas" title="University of Santo Tomas">University of Santo Tomas</a> that houses the Conservatory of Music, College of Tourism and Hospitality Management, College of Education, and UST Education High School is named in his honor. <a href="/wiki/Saint_Albert_the_Great_Science_Academy" title="Saint Albert the Great Science Academy">The Saint Albert the Great Science Academy</a> in <a href="/wiki/San_Carlos,_Pangasinan" title="San Carlos, Pangasinan">San Carlos City</a>, <a href="/wiki/Pangasinan" title="Pangasinan">Pangasinan</a>, which offers preschool, elementary and high school education, takes pride in having St. Albert as their patron saint. Its main building was named Albertus Magnus Hall in 2008. San Alberto Magno Academy in <a href="/wiki/Tubao" title="Tubao">Tubao</a>, <a href="/wiki/La_Union" title="La Union">La Union</a> is also dedicated in his honor. This century-old Catholic high school continues to live on its vision-mission up to this day, offering Senior High school courses.
</p><p>Due to his contributions to natural philosophy, the bacterium <i>Agrobacterium albertimagni</i>,<sup id="cite_ref-47" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-47">&#91;46&#93;</a></sup> the plant species <i><a href="/wiki/Alberta_magna" title="Alberta magna">Alberta magna</a></i>, the crustacean <i>Bodigiella albertimagni</i>,<sup id="cite_ref-48" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-48">&#91;47&#93;</a></sup> the fossil brachiopod <i>Albasphe albertimagni</i>,<sup id="cite_ref-49" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-49">&#91;48&#93;</a></sup> and the asteroid <a href="/wiki/20006_Albertus_Magnus" class="mw-redirect" title="20006 Albertus Magnus">20006 Albertus Magnus</a> were named after him.
</p><p>Numerous Catholic elementary and secondary schools are named for him, including schools in Toronto; Calgary; Cologne; and Dayton, Ohio.
</p><p>The <a href="/wiki/Albertus_(typeface)" title="Albertus (typeface)">Albertus typeface</a> is named after him.<sup id="cite_ref-50" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-50">&#91;49&#93;</a></sup>
At the <a href="/wiki/University_of_Notre_Dame" title="University of Notre Dame">University of Notre Dame</a> du Lac in <a href="/wiki/Notre_Dame,_Indiana" title="Notre Dame, Indiana">Notre Dame, Indiana</a>, the <a href="/wiki/Zahm_Hall_(University_of_Notre_Dame)" class="mw-redirect" title="Zahm Hall (University of Notre Dame)">Zahm Hall</a> Chapel is dedicated to St. Albert the Great. <a href="/wiki/John_Augustine_Zahm" title="John Augustine Zahm">Fr. John Zahm, C.S.C.</a>, after whom the men's residence hall is named, looked to St. Albert's example of using religion to illumine scientific discovery. Fr. Zahm's work with the Bible and evolution is sometimes seen as a continuation of St. Albert's legacy.
</p><p>The second largest student's fraternity of the Netherlands, located in the city of <a href="/wiki/Groningen" title="Groningen">Groningen</a>, is named <a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://www.albertus.nl">Albertus Magnus</a>, in honor of the saint.
</p><p>The Colegio Cientifico y Artistico de San Alberto, Hopelawn, New Jersey, USA with a sister school in Nueva Ecija, Philippines was founded in 1986 in honor of him who thought and taught that religion, the sciences and the arts may be advocated as subjects which should not contradict each other but should support one another to achieve wisdom and reason.
</p><p>The Vosloorus catholic parish (located in Vosloorus Extension One, Ekurhuleni, Gauteng, South Africa) is named after the saint.
</p><p>The <a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://www.kath-ka-hardt.de/html/st_antonius_egg_leo.html">catholic parish</a> in Leopoldshafen, near <a href="/wiki/Karlsruhe" title="Karlsruhe">Karlsruhe</a> in Germany is also named after him also considering the huge research center of the <a href="/wiki/Karlsruhe_Institute_of_Technology" title="Karlsruhe Institute of Technology">Karlsruhe Institute of Technology</a> nearby, as he is the patron saint of scientists.
</p><p>Since the death of <a href="/wiki/Albert_I_of_Belgium" title="Albert I of Belgium">King Albert I</a>, the <a href="/wiki/King%27s_Feast" title="King&#39;s Feast">King's Feast</a> is celebrated in Belgium on Albert's feast day.
</p><p><a href="/wiki/St_Albert%27s_Catholic_Chaplaincy,_Edinburgh" title="St Albert&#39;s Catholic Chaplaincy, Edinburgh">Edinburgh's Catholic Chaplaincy</a> serving the city's universities is named after St Albert.
</p><p><a href="/wiki/Sant%27Alberto_Magno" title="Sant&#39;Alberto Magno">Sant'Alberto Magno</a> is a <a href="/wiki/Titular_church" title="Titular church">titular church</a> in <a href="/wiki/Rome" title="Rome">Rome</a>.
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Bibliography">Bibliography</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Albertus_Magnus&amp;action=edit&amp;section=12" title="Edit section: Bibliography">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Translations">Translations</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Albertus_Magnus&amp;action=edit&amp;section=13" title="Edit section: Translations">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<ul><li><i>On Fate</i>, (De Fato) translated by D.P. Curtin (Philadelphia, PA: Dalcassian Publishing Company: 2023).</li>
<li><i>On the Body of the Lord,</i> translated by Sr. Albert Marie Surmanski, OP (Washington, D.C.: Catholic University of America Press: 2017).</li>
<li><i>On the Causes of the Properties of the Elements</i>, translated by Irven M. Resnick (Milwaukee: Marquette University Press, 2010) [translation of <i>Liber de causis proprietatum elementorum</i>]</li>
<li><i>Questions concerning Aristotle's on Animals</i>, translated by Irven M. Resnick and Kenneth F. Kitchell Jr. (Washington, D.C.: Catholic University of America Press, 2008) [translation of <i>Quaestiones super De animalibus</i>]</li>
<li><i>The Cardinal Virtues: Aquinas, Albert, and Philip the Chancellor</i>, translated by R. E. Houser (Toronto: Pontifical Institute of Mediæval Studies, 2004) [contains translations of <i>Parisian Summa, part six: On the good</i> and <i>Commentary on the Sentences of Peter Lombard</i>, book 3, dist. 33 &amp; 36]</li>
<li><i>The Commentary of Albertus Magnus on Book 1 of Euclid's Elements of Geometry</i>, edited by Anthony Lo Bello (Boston: Brill Academic Publishers, 2003) [translation of <i>Priumus Euclidis cum commento Alberti</i>]</li>
<li><i>On Animals: A Medieval Summa Zoologica</i>, translated by Kenneth F. Kitchell Jr. and Irven Michael Resnick (Baltimore; London: Johns Hopkins University Press, 1999) [translation of <i>De animalibus</i>]</li>
<li>Paola Zambelli, <i>The Speculum Astronomiae and Its Enigma: Astrology, Theology, and Science in Albertus Magnus and His Contemporaries</i> (Dordrecht; Boston: Kluwer Academic Publishers, 1992) [includes Latin text and English translation of <i>Speculum astronomiae</i>]</li>
<li><i>Albert &amp; Thomas: Selected Writings</i>, translated by Simon Tugwell, Classics of Western Spirituality (New York: Paulist Press, 1988) [contains translation of <i>Super Dionysii Mysticam theologiam</i>]</li>
<li><i>On Union with God</i>, translated by a Benedictine of Princethorpe Priory (London: Burns Oates &amp; Washbourne, 1911) [reprinted as (Felinfach: Llanerch Enterprises, 1991) and (London: Continuum, 2000)] [translation of <i>De adherendo Deo</i>]</li></ul>
